## 🙋‍♂️ Why?

1. To help you pick a minifier that fits your needs
2. To promote JS minifiers and document their performances
3. To encourage healthy competition and improvement amongst minifiers

## 👟 Methodology

- Each minifier is executed in its own process with a 10s timeout for the whole run, including validation
- Artifact integrity is verified by a test before and after minification
- Each minifier is minimally configured (sourcemaps & comments disabled), comparing out-of-the-box experience
- Minifier upgrade PRs are automated via [WhiteSource Renovate](https://www.whitesourcesoftware.com/free-developer-tools/renovate/)
- Benchmarks are updated on every PR from this repository via [GitHub Actions](https://github.com/privatenumber/minification-benchmarks/actions/workflows/benchmark.yml)
- The raw benchmark data is available in [`/packages/data/data/data.json`](/packages/data/data/data.json)


## ⏱ Metrics

### Ranking

For each benchmark, minifiers are ranked by a score that reflects the best balance of trade-offs.

The score combines minzipped size and minification time, starting from an 85% size, 15% time split since size is usually the top priority. A [logistic sigmoid function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmoid_function) smoothly increases the time weight based on the ratio to the median time, up to a 50/50 split. This avoids harsh cutoffs while still penalizing extreme slowness.

Both size and time are scaled using [min-max normalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_scaling#Rescaling_%28min–max_normalization%29) so they can be compared fairly. The result is a context-aware score that helps surface the best overall choice—but make sure to check each column directly if size or speed matters more to you.


#### Minified size

Size of the minified output.

#### Minzipped size

Size of the minified output with [Gzip compression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gzip).

For minifiers, this measures how compressable the output is.

For users, this measures network transfer size, which is usually the metric that matters most.

#### Time

How long minification took (average of 5 runs). Each time is annotated with a multiplier relative to the fastest minifier.

## ⚔️ Minifier showdown

> [!NOTE]
> 🤖 This analysis is AI generated. See below for the system prompt.

<!-- aiAnalysis:start -->
**Champion: @swc/core**, with 7 of 12 round wins by the ranking above.

Three... two... one... compress! Welcome back to the Minification Grand Prix, where twelve rounds of ruthless byte-slaying separate the sharp from the sluggish. We ran the gauntlet from a featherweight react bundle to a monstrous 1.88 MB typescript compiler. Two contenders didn't survive the d3 turn. One veteran refused to age. And a champion emerged by doing everything well at once.

### Best minifier

@swc/core takes the crown with 7 round wins out of 12, and it earned every single one. Here's the thing about swc: it is never the fastest tool in the pit, and it doesn't always grab the absolute smallest gzip. It does something better. It shows up to every round with near-best compression at a fraction of the cost.

Look at jquery and vue: best gzip in the field, 30.86 KB and 42.72 KB, delivered in 27 and 37 milliseconds. Look at three and echarts: best gzip again, 158.70 KB and 321.03 KB, on artifacts where the competition needs a nap and a sandwich. And then there's victory, the round that tells the whole story. uglify-js squeezed out 157.44 KB — best size of the night — but needed 3,508 ms to do it. swc landed at 157.70 KB in 155 ms. That's 0.26 KB, bought with more than twenty times the patience. In a CI pipeline, that's not a trade-off. That's a gift.

Yes, the clock climbs on the biggest artifacts — 415 ms on echarts, 521 ms on antd. But swc still took best gzip on echarts and stayed within a whisker of the leaders everywhere else. No drift, no panic, no sacrificed metric. Seven balanced wins across small packages and giants alike. Champion, deservedly.

### Honorable mentions

oxc-minify, the runner-up with 5 wins, is the story of the season. Fastest in nearly every round — 2 ms on react, 4 ms on moment — while compressing like a heavyweight. And when the files got huge, oxc got stronger: best size AND fastest time on terser (121.88 KB in 18 ms — yes, it out-minified the minifier), antd (450.94 KB in 139 ms), and typescript (846.30 KB in 305 ms). A clean sweep of the biggest stages. Watch this one.

uglify-js, the immortal veteran, still owns the pure compression crown on react, moment, lodash, d3, and victory. Five best-gzip performances. The catch? The clock. Three and a half seconds on victory, nearly two on d3. If your build has time to spare and every byte is sacred, uglify remains the monk of minification.

@cminify/cminify-linux-x64 is the pure sprinter. Fastest on every big round — a blistering 73 ms on the typescript beast. But it leaves bytes bleeding on the track: 40% shaved on typescript versus oxc's 55%. A niche weapon for when speed is the only scoreboard that matters.

terser turned in quietly professional shifts, snagging honorable mentions on react and moment. @tdewolff/minify flashed genuine speed with respectable sizes on jquery, vue, and typescript. esbuild popped onto the reel with a solid 331.62 KB on echarts. Meanwhile, google-closure-compiler and bun never cracked the highlights this season — outrun, outgunned, but still on the grid.

### Eliminated

- babel-minify: crashed out on d3 with "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'add')". A minifier that can't finish the course can't win it.
- tedivm/jshrink: choked on d3's regexes and threw a PHP RuntimeException. Unclosed pattern, closed championship hopes.

### Closing remarks

What a season. A champion that balanced everything, a runner-up that redefined fast, and a graybeard proving old-school compression still bites. One caveat before you crown your own winner: this Grand Prix scores gzip size and speed only. Real-world adoption also weighs install size, API ergonomics, community, and correctness on your actual code. Take these contenders for a lap around your own pipeline — and may your bundles be ever tiny.
